72-The other end of the front deserves its own sentence, because the rest of this
73-paper is negative and a reader could close it thinking the answer is to do

81-\texttt{gen\_nocontrol.py} reports 73.8\% on that convention) to 1.6\%, and its segment SLO is 30\% in the first
82-tenth of the run and exactly zero in each of the nine after that. Uncontrolled
83-admission does not degrade gracefully; it destroys the cache and then serves
84-almost nobody. The question this paper addresses is therefore not whether to
85-admit selectively, but which selective rule to use.

87-Two features of the front are worth noting before the negative results.
88-The envelope is steep between 209 and 230 minutes (0.63\,pp per minute) and
89-shallow thereafter (0.15\,pp per minute), so the cost of buying SLO by
90-retreating from the fast end is high. And $W$ is not monotone in concurrency:
91-five sessions (260.9\,min) is \emph{slower} than four (249.9\,min)---the same
92-configuration family, no host cache on either side---because the per-step cost
93-rises faster than the request count does. We return to this
94-non-monotonicity in \S\ref{sec:model}, where it turns out to be the first
95:appearance of the mechanism that drives the whole frontier.

321-\subsection{What the two mechanisms explain, arm by arm}
322-\label{sec:results:attribution}
323-
324-We have offered two mechanisms, and the honest thing is to say what each one
325-covers. For every controller arm we took the static cap closest to it in wall
326-clock and compared their occupancy and their step cost. An arm is attributed to
327-the unused-memory mechanism if it runs fewer than 92\% of its comparison's
328:requests, and to the step-cost mechanism if it runs a comparable number---%
329-within 8\%---at a measurably higher cost (more than 8\% higher $\tau_1$).
330-Everything else is a residual. The rule reads the running count and
331-$\tau_1$ only; it does not consult occupancy, so the ``unused memory'' label
332-is licensed by the running count alone---though the three arms it lands on also
333-sit well below their comparison's occupancy (54.5, 56.2 and 62.1 against
334-74.3\%), which is why we let the name stand. The comparability clause is load-bearing and we
335-state it because an earlier version of this table did not enforce it, which
336-classified every arm that ran \emph{more} requests than its comparison as
337-step-cost purely because $\tau$ rises with the running count.

228-\paragraph{Where the mechanism stops.} With HiCache enabled the
229-correspondence breaks---and it breaks in a way that confirms the mechanism
230-rather than undermining it. The device cache misses as much as before:
231-\texttt{cap4\_hc} misses 22.4\,M tokens on the device, more than the
232-device-only \texttt{cap4} misses. But only 9.1\,M of that is recomputed; the
233-other 13.3\,M is served from the host tier. Across the nineteen host-tier
234:runs the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M tokens each, and across the five device-only
235-runs it serves almost nothing---0.006\% to 0.25\% of the device miss, the rest
236-of which is recomputed on all five. Recomputation is still what the device cannot
237-absorb; the host tier is a second absorber in front of it.

441:The obvious remedy---keep more sessions live so that someone is always
442:ready---is what \texttt{bres} does, and the clean measurement of it says the
443:remedy works and does not pay. \texttt{bres} cuts idle time by 9.5 minutes
444:against its own baseline, and its busy time falls as well, by 15.5---so the
445:arm simply finishes 25.4 minutes earlier. What it does not do is improve the
446-criterion: its SLO falls 3.9\,pp and its distance below the static front is
447-unchanged (\S\ref{sec:results}). Recovering idle time is not the same act as
448-buying a better operating point, and here the one does not produce the other.
449-We are reporting a correction in this paragraph, not just a refinement: an
450-earlier draft had idle falling while busy rose, leaving the run slower, and
451-that shape came from a measurement we have since found was taken under host
452-co-tenancy (\S\ref{sec:repro}). Idle is recoverable; it is recoverable at a
453-price that does not move the verdict.
454-The structural remedy would be to make the engine do work during a wait, but
455-there is no work to do: the session's next round does not exist until its tool
456-returns.

paper/sec_model.tex-228-\paragraph{Where the mechanism stops.} With HiCache enabled the
paper/sec_model.tex-229-correspondence breaks---and it breaks in a way that confirms the mechanism
paper/sec_model.tex-230-rather than undermining it. The device cache misses as much as before:
paper/sec_model.tex-231-\texttt{cap4\_hc} misses 22.4\,M tokens on the device, more than the
paper/sec_model.tex-232-device-only \texttt{cap4} misses. But only 9.1\,M of that is recomputed; the
paper/sec_model.tex:233:other 13.3\,M is served from the host tier. Across the nineteen host-tier
paper/sec_model.tex-234-runs the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M tokens each, and across the five device-only
paper/sec_model.tex-235-runs it serves almost nothing---0.006\% to 0.25\% of the device miss, the rest
paper/sec_model.tex-236-of which is recomputed on all five. Recomputation is still what the device cannot
paper/sec_model.tex:237:absorb; the host tier is a second absorber in front of it.

paper/sec_model.tex-244-$\text{reuse} + \text{recomputed} = \text{prompt}$, with the prompt fixed at

paper/sec_model.tex-249-reusable prefix (\texttt{prefix\_len}) over all 999 rounds of
paper/sec_model.tex-250-\texttt{replay\_pois200\_l04.csv} gives 21{,}540{,}371 tokens against a prompt
paper/sec_model.tex-251-total of 28{,}526{,}871, so no policy can reuse more than 75.5\% of the
paper/sec_model.tex-252-prompt. That is a property of the workload. It is also a bound rather than a
paper/sec_model.tex-253-description: \emph{no configuration we ran reaches it}, and the best realizes
paper/sec_model.tex-254-92.5\%, so the gap between ``planned'' and ``achieved'' exists even in the
paper/sec_model.tex-255-easiest case. What varies across configurations is how much of the bound is
paper/sec_model.tex:256:realized, and that is where the tier enters.

paper/sec_model.tex-312-Device occupancy does not explain the split. \texttt{cap3\_fixed} and

paper/sec_model.tex:316:device pool is but what happens when it overflows: with no host tier an
paper/sec_model.tex-317-evicted prefix is gone and must be recomputed; with one it is demoted and read
paper/sec_model.tex:318:back. The pool is the binding resource, the tier sets the pool, and the tier
paper/sec_model.tex-319-therefore sets whether added concurrency is paid for in recomputation. The
paper/sec_model.tex:320:caveat is that this pair is two runs on two different days and two different
paper/sec_model.tex-321-GPUs, not a paired experiment; \S\ref{sec:repro} states what we did to rule

paper/sec_model.tex-324-\S\ref{sec:results} when it lands; we do not use it here.

paper/sec_model.tex:360:paragraph claimed. Across the nineteen host-tier runs recomputation spans

paper/sec_model.tex-362-27.0--36.2\,ms. Three of those runs

paper/sec_model.tex-364-\texttt{F:breshz1} 36.2\,ms) were
paper/sec_model.tex-365-measured while three other engine instances shared the host, which inflates
paper/sec_model.tex-366-per-step cost by 24--41\% and leaves the volume columns untouched
paper/sec_model.tex-367-(\S\ref{sec:repro}); they are being re-measured and we do not use their
paper/sec_model.tex-368-timing. A fourth was re-measured clean and is in the set above with its new

paper/sec_model.tex-370-unexplained at all: it tracks their recomputation closely (a straight line

paper/sec_model.tex-375-result does not rest on it either way: the cancellation is measured directly,

paper/sec_background.tex-33-\subsection{Engine and the binding constraint}
paper/sec_background.tex-34-
paper/sec_background.tex-35-We use SGLang v0.5.10 \cite{zheng2024sglang} with Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct
paper/sec_background.tex-36-\cite{qwen2coder} extended to 96K context,
paper/sec_background.tex-37-on one RTX 3090 (24\,GB). KV is paged \cite{kwon2023pagedattention}; at \texttt{mem-fraction-static} 0.85
paper/sec_background.tex:38:the pool is 101{,}432 tokens. Hierarchical caching (HiCache), with a host tier of twice the device pool, is
paper/sec_background.tex-39-enabled on the configurations marked \emph{+HC} and on every \sys{} run; the
paper/sec_background.tex:40:five plain static caps run without it. We keep both because the host tier turns
paper/sec_background.tex-41-out to change the mechanism (\S\ref{sec:model}). Every run is guarded: if the pool does not
paper/sec_background.tex-42-come out at $101{,}432 \pm 20$---which happens when another tenant holds
paper/sec_background.tex-43-memory on the card---the run is aborted rather than recorded, because SGLang
paper/sec_background.tex-44-sizes the pool from free memory at startup and a shrunken pool silently
paper/sec_background.tex-45-produces incomparable numbers.
paper/sec_background.tex-46-
paper/sec_background.tex-47-The relevant consequence is that the pool holds only about four median
paper/sec_background.tex:48:sessions. Concurrency is therefore capacity-bound, not scheduler-bound, and
paper/sec_background.tex-49-this is the premise on which everything below rests.
paper/sec_background.tex-50-
paper/sec_background.tex-51-\subsection{The controller under test}
paper/sec_background.tex-52-
paper/sec_background.tex-53-\sys{} is an admission controller that reads engine telemetry and issues
paper/sec_background.tex-54-\emph{named} permits rather than a scalar cap: a session is admitted by
paper/sec_background.tex-55-appearing in a permit file, and the runner refuses to start a round for a
paper/sec_background.tex-56-session that is not listed. This design choice matters for the comparison,
paper/sec_background.tex-57-because it is what allows per-session actions at all---including the pause
paper/sec_background.tex-58-primitive we exploit in \S\ref{sec:results:gate}. It also means that a session,
paper/sec_background.tex-59-once admitted, holds its permit for its entire lifetime, so the controller
paper/sec_background.tex:60:never frees capacity by discarding a session's state---it admits fewer sessions
paper/sec_background.tex-61-instead. That is a statement about the \emph{policy}, not about the memory: the
paper/sec_background.tex-62-engine's radix cache still evicts parked prefixes under pressure, and we
paper/sec_background.tex-63-measure that it does and that they are recomputed (\S\ref{sec:model}). We
paper/sec_background.tex-64-return to this distinction in \S\ref{sec:discussion}, because it bounds how far
paper/sec_background.tex-65-our negative result generalises.
paper/sec_background.tex-66-
paper/sec_background.tex-67-Each control cycle (2\,s) the controller computes
paper/sec_background.tex-68-\begin{equation}
paper/sec_background.tex-69-  \Pi \;=\; \max(\rho, \textstyle\sum_i c_i) \;+\; \textstyle\sum_i g_i ,
paper/sec_background.tex-70-  \label{eq:proj}

paper/sec_background.tex-83-with and without HiCache. We compare on two axes: end-to-end wall clock
paper/sec_background.tex-84-(time from the first submission to the last completion) and SLO attainment
paper/sec_background.tex-85-(fraction of requests whose time-to-first-token is under 10\,s).
paper/sec_background.tex-86-
paper/sec_background.tex-87-The verdict criterion deserves care, because ``faster than cap-$N$'' is not
paper/sec_background.tex-88-the question. The question is whether a policy reaches a point \emph{above}
paper/sec_background.tex-89-the static front. A point that lies merely between two static caps is not a
paper/sec_background.tex-90-win: mixing cap-$N$ and cap-$M$ by time slice approximates any point on the
paper/sec_background.tex-91-line between them. We flag this segment argument as an argument, not a
paper/sec_background.tex:92:measurement---we did not run the time-sliced policies, and carrying queue state
paper/sec_background.tex-93-and cache contents across a switch could bend the line. It is the criterion we
paper/sec_background.tex-94-apply, and it is the stricter of the two we could have used---stricter than
paper/sec_background.tex-95-comparing each controller against the discrete static points alone, because the
paper/sec_background.tex-96-line sits above them. We state the direction plainly rather than claim it as a
paper/sec_background.tex-97-caution: this choice makes our negative result \emph{easier} to reach, not
paper/sec_background.tex-98-harder. Our best arm is $0.93$\,pp below the line and would be $+3.7$\,pp
paper/sec_background.tex-99-against the nearest static point below it in wall clock (\texttt{cap3}+HiCache's
paper/sec_background.tex-100-first run, 81.1\% at 236.4 minutes; $+2.9$ would be the comparison against that
paper/sec_background.tex-101-anchor's repeat at 81.9\%, which is the one we displaced it with). So we take the
paper/sec_background.tex-102-upper-left

paper/main.tex-30-The Memory Tier Sets the Cost of Concurrency in Agentic LLM Serving}
paper/main.tex-31-
paper/main.tex-32-\author{\IEEEauthorblockN{Anonymous Submission}}
paper/main.tex-33-
paper/main.tex-34-\maketitle
paper/main.tex-35-
paper/main.tex-36-\begin{abstract}
paper/main.tex-37-Agentic LLM sessions are long, multi-round jobs whose KV footprint grows every
paper/main.tex-38-round, and on a memory-constrained accelerator the pool is the binding
paper/main.tex-39-resource. Admission control therefore looks like the obvious lever: a
paper/main.tex:40:controller that reads engine telemetry and admits on measured capacity should
paper/main.tex-41-beat a static cap. We give a measurement that says how much
paper/main.tex-42-room there is, and show what happens when you build against it. Throughput during busy time is
paper/main.tex-43-$\overline{R}/\tau$---running requests over per-step cost---so raising
paper/main.tex-44-concurrency by a factor $f$ buys $f^{1-\epsilon}$, where
paper/main.tex-45-$\epsilon = \mathrm{d}\ln\tau / \mathrm{d}\ln\overline{R}$. Near zero, busy-time throughput keeps
paper/main.tex-46-converting; near one it does not. It is a property of the workload \emph{and}
paper/main.tex-47-the engine configuration, not of the controller, and what sets it is the
paper/main.tex:48:memory tier. The trace bounds reuse at 75.5\% of its prompt tokens, and no
paper/main.tex-49-configuration we ran reaches that bound---the best realizes 92.5\% of it.
paper/main.tex:50:What the tier decides is how much of the bound survives added concurrency:
paper/main.tex-51-device-only, realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% as the running count
paper/main.tex-52-rises from 1.00 to 1.84 and the shortfall appears as recomputation (8.6\,M to
paper/main.tex:53:20.7\,M tokens); with a host tier the static points hold at 90.2--92.4\%
paper/main.tex:54:between 1.25 and 1.73, and across the nineteen host-tier runs that completed
paper/main.tex-55-this trace realization never falls below 72.8\%, while the \emph{device} cache
paper/main.tex-56-misses more. Two configurations at the
paper/main.tex-57-same running count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of
paper/main.tex-58-the bound, so it is not how full the pool is but what happens when it
paper/main.tex:59:overflows---though that pair is two runs on two days, so we also have a
paper/main.tex:60:capacity control in the queue that changes the pool size alone on one card; we
paper/main.tex:61:report it when it lands. Our controllers run on the host-tier configuration, where
paper/main.tex-62-$\epsilon$ stays below one across their whole operating range, so the ceiling
paper/main.tex-63-we measure on device-only caps is not what bounded them. It bounds what added concurrency is worth, not what a
paper/main.tex-64-whole run achieves---idle time and the SLO axis are separate. We measure $\epsilon$ on a
paper/main.tex-65-memory-bound agentic replay (200 sessions, one 24\,GB GPU, a pool that holds
paper/main.tex-66-four median sessions) and get $0.38$ to $0.61$ depending on which step cost a
paper/main.tex-67-system can observe: every 10\% of added concurrency buys 4--6\% of throughput.
paper/main.tex-68-A second, real agent trace (117 Claude sessions, same engine and pool)
paper/main.tex-69-reproduces both measurements in direction, neither in level: realization falls
paper/main.tex-70-there too, and the elasticity there also turns positive---rising to 1.0 where
paper/main.tex-71-the main trace's reaches 1.15---at a different running count and by an amount
--
paper/main.tex-90-The question is which selective rule, not whether to be selective. Two
paper/main.tex-91-measurable effects, rather than one we can tune away, account for part of why
paper/main.tex-92-our variants fail---the arm-by-arm attribution of
paper/main.tex-93-\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} attributes four of the twelve controller
paper/main.tex-94-arms we tested, leaves five with a step cost that no matched comparison can
paper/main.tex-95-confirm, and leaves three as residuals we can measure and cannot yet
paper/main.tex-96-explain. The single step-cost attribution it does make is on a run flagged
paper/main.tex-97-for host co-tenancy but not measurably damaged by it, and we say which is
paper/main.tex-98-which rather than counting it. They hold
paper/main.tex-99-nearly half
paper/main.tex:100:the pool idle while sessions queue---their own headroom estimate on the base
paper/main.tex-101-configuration is 1.79$\times$ the truth (1.32--1.90$\times$ across the
paper/main.tex-102-sixteen controller runs that finished on this trace, and 1.01--4.11$\times$
paper/main.tex-103-over every finished controller run we logged)---and the per-step elasticity measured on the
paper/main.tex-104-configuration they actually run stays below one across their whole operating
paper/main.tex-105-range, so the engine had room they did not take: the ceiling we measure on
paper/main.tex:106:device-only caps does not survive on the host-tier configuration the
paper/main.tex-107-controllers use. The wall
paper/main.tex-108-clock splits into busy and idle time; admission policy moves only the running
paper/main.tex-109-count, and $\tau$ rises with it. The idle component (7.3--41.9\% of the
paper/main.tex-110-sampled span) comes from gaps the tool waits set---an agent session issues no
paper/main.tex-111-request while a tool runs,
paper/main.tex-112-and that wait averages 16.85\,s measured over the controller's run, against the
paper/main.tex-113-15.82\,s the trace demands---on the static cap of one the two match exactly, so
paper/main.tex-114-we attribute the controller's extra 1.03\,s to its own admission delay---not
paper/main.tex-115-the sub-second figure the trace's median suggests---though how many such gaps a run collects is partly the policy's,
paper/main.tex-116-since a policy sets how many sessions stay live. On device-only configurations we can account for the rise token for
paper/main.tex:117:token; on host-tier ones part of it is co-tenancy we found late and part we
paper/main.tex-118-still cannot attribute, and we say which is which. We also report the six of
paper/main.tex-119-our own claims we retracted along the way, and the boundary of the verdict: at
paper/main.tex-120-a loose latency threshold one variant does cross the front, by a margin we
paper/main.tex-121-cannot resolve against run-to-run noise.

paper/sec_intro.tex-23-
paper/sec_intro.tex-24-\subsection{What we measured}
paper/sec_intro.tex-25-
paper/sec_intro.tex-26-We serve a replay of 200 real coding-agent sessions on a single RTX 3090
paper/sec_intro.tex-27-(Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, 96K context, SGLang, a 101{,}432-token KV pool) and compare
paper/sec_intro.tex-28-a family of admission policies against a sweep of static concurrency caps.
paper/sec_intro.tex-29-Nine controller variants, spanning four mechanistically distinct ideas, all
paper/sec_intro.tex-30-land below the static Pareto front, and so does a reimplementation of an
paper/sec_intro.tex-31-independent congestion-based controller from the literature
paper/sec_intro.tex:32:(Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier}). The best of them misses by under a point---inside the
paper/sec_intro.tex-33-$\pm1.0$\,pp band we call a tie, but on the below side of it---and the closest
paper/sec_intro.tex-34-arm we repeated misses by 0.9 with its repeat at 5.6.
paper/sec_intro.tex-35-
paper/sec_intro.tex-36-\begin{figure}[tb]
paper/sec_intro.tex-37-  \centering
paper/sec_intro.tex:38:  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig1_frontier.pdf}
paper/sec_intro.tex-39-  \caption{Every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO) plane. The grey line
paper/sec_intro.tex-40-  is the upper-left envelope of the static points---drawn from the data, so it
paper/sec_intro.tex-41-  uses each configuration's \emph{better} run where a repeat exists, which is
paper/sec_intro.tex-42-  why \texttt{cap3}+HiCache's 236-minute run sits below the line rather than on
paper/sec_intro.tex-43-  it; a policy below or on it has
paper/sec_intro.tex-44-  not beaten a static cap, because time-slicing two caps approximates any
paper/sec_intro.tex-45-  point on the segment between them. Nine \sys{} variants plus a
paper/sec_intro.tex-46-  reimplementation of an independent controller, none above at the 10\,s
paper/sec_intro.tex-47-  latency threshold used throughout; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports how far
paper/sec_intro.tex-48-  that verdict is conditional on the threshold. The five points drawn with a
paper/sec_intro.tex-49-  hollow ring were measured while three or more other engine instances shared
paper/sec_intro.tex-50-  the host (\S\ref{sec:repro}); on three of them the per-request decode
paper/sec_intro.tex-51-  interval is inflated by 24--41\% and they are not comparable with the rest,
paper/sec_intro.tex-52-  on the other two the flag is a window that left no measurable trace. The

paper/sec_intro.tex:55:  \label{fig:frontier}

paper/sec_intro.tex-57-
paper/sec_intro.tex-58-\subsection{Why}
paper/sec_intro.tex-59-
paper/sec_intro.tex-60-We decompose the wall clock into \emph{busy} time, during which the engine has
paper/sec_intro.tex-61-at least one request in flight, and \emph{idle} time, during which it has
paper/sec_intro.tex-62-none. The busy component is governed by $W_{\text{busy}} \approx
paper/sec_intro.tex-63-(G/\overline{R})\,\tau$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:busy}), where $\overline{R}$ is the mean
paper/sec_intro.tex-64-number of running requests, $G$ the generated tokens and $\tau$ the per-step
paper/sec_intro.tex-65-cost. An admission policy moves $\overline{R}$, and on our configurations it
paper/sec_intro.tex-66-moves little else---though \S\ref{sec:model} gives two arms where the busy and
paper/sec_intro.tex-67-idle components move in \emph{opposite} directions, which is why the split is
paper/sec_intro.tex-68-worth making at all.
paper/sec_intro.tex-69-The difficulty is that $\tau$ is not constant in $\overline{R}$: across our
paper/sec_intro.tex-70-runs $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.7\,ms, and on the device-only family, raising the
paper/sec_intro.tex-71-busy-time running count from 1.00 to 1.84 absorbs a third of what the extra
paper/sec_intro.tex-72-concurrency buys.
paper/sec_intro.tex-73-
paper/sec_intro.tex-74-The reason $\tau$ moves is recomputation, and whether recomputation happens is
paper/sec_intro.tex:75:decided by the memory tier rather than by the policy. A session's prefix is
paper/sec_intro.tex-76-worth reusing only while it is still resident, so prefix \emph{reuse} is
paper/sec_intro.tex-77-bounded by the workload---in our trace, 75.5\% of the prompt tokens, a bound
paper/sec_intro.tex-78-no configuration we ran reaches. How much of that bound is realized is where
paper/sec_intro.tex:79:the tier enters. With the KV pool on the device alone, realization falls as
paper/sec_intro.tex-80-concurrency rises---92.5\% of the bound at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00
paper/sec_intro.tex-81-down to 36.4\% at 1.84---and the shortfall appears as recomputation, 8.6\,M to
paper/sec_intro.tex:82:20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier in front of it the decline disappears: over the
paper/sec_intro.tex-83-running counts our controllers occupy, the static points fall only from 92.4\%
paper/sec_intro.tex:84:to 90.2\%, and the worst of the nineteen host-tier runs---the one that disables
paper/sec_intro.tex-85-every throttle---still realizes 72.8\%, where the nearest device-only point
paper/sec_intro.tex-86-(the cap of four, at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.65) manages 60.7\%, while
paper/sec_intro.tex-87-the \emph{device} misses more. Two configurations at the same running
paper/sec_intro.tex-88-count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound
paper/sec_intro.tex-89-respectively (\S\ref{sec:model}); what separates them is not how full the pool
paper/sec_intro.tex-90-is but what happens when it overflows. That pair also differs in date and in
paper/sec_intro.tex:91:GPU, so a capacity control that changes the pool size alone on one card is
paper/sec_intro.tex:92:queued behind the re-runs; \S\ref{sec:results} reports it when it lands.
paper/sec_intro.tex-93-That is the finding we would carry to another system first, and it is why this
paper/sec_intro.tex-94-paper's answer to its own title is a property of the deployment rather than of
paper/sec_intro.tex-95-the controller.
paper/sec_intro.tex-96-
paper/sec_intro.tex-97-The idle component is structural rather than a control failure. A session in a
paper/sec_intro.tex-98-tool wait issues no request, so when all live sessions happen to be waiting,
paper/sec_intro.tex-99-the engine is genuinely empty. The stretches are short in the middle and long
paper/sec_intro.tex-100-in the tail---mean 20.9\,s---and the tail is what a scheduler feels: 69.9\%
paper/sec_intro.tex-101-of all idle \emph{time} sits in stretches over 20\,s, an order of magnitude
paper/sec_intro.tex-102-above the 2\,s control cycle. (The median is not a good summary here: it moves
--
paper/sec_intro.tex-104-contributes a fifth of the stretches.) Crucially, the tool wait itself is much longer than
paper/sec_intro.tex-105-the trace's median suggests: the median inter-round gap is 0.35\,s, but the
paper/sec_intro.tex-106-\emph{mean} is 15.8--16.9\,s with 22\% of gaps over ten seconds. A controller
paper/sec_intro.tex-107-that
paper/sec_intro.tex-108-reads only the median concludes, as we initially did, that sessions never
paper/sec_intro.tex-109-leave and therefore that there is no idle time to recover. There is.
paper/sec_intro.tex-110-
paper/sec_intro.tex-111-\subsection{Contributions}
paper/sec_intro.tex-112-
paper/sec_intro.tex-113-\begin{itemize}
paper/sec_intro.tex:114:  \item \textbf{We identify the memory tier as what decides how much of the
paper/sec_intro.tex-115-        achievable prefix reuse survives added concurrency, and therefore how
paper/sec_intro.tex-116-        much a controller has to gain by admitting more.} The two senses of
paper/sec_intro.tex-117-        ``room'' need separating, and \S\ref{sec:xtrace} is what separates
paper/sec_intro.tex:118:        them: the tier bounds the \emph{local} return on added concurrency
paper/sec_intro.tex-119-        (busy-time throughput), and it does not by itself decide whether a
paper/sec_intro.tex-120-        controller wins \emph{end to end}, where idle time and the SLO axis
paper/sec_intro.tex-121-        also move. Our second workload is the demonstration---it has no host
paper/sec_intro.tex:122:        tier and its controller runs at low concurrency (1.22), the two
paper/sec_intro.tex-123-        conditions this section associates with room, and it still does not win
paper/sec_intro.tex-124-        (\S\ref{sec:xtrace}). The claim here is the local one. The trace
paper/sec_intro.tex-125-        bounds reuse at
paper/sec_intro.tex-126-        75.5\% of its prompt tokens, and no configuration we ran reaches that
paper/sec_intro.tex:127:        bound---the best realizes 92.5\% of it. What the tier decides is how
paper/sec_intro.tex-128-        much of the bound is realized as concurrency rises: device-only,
paper/sec_intro.tex-129-        realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% between
paper/sec_intro.tex-130-        $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00 and 1.84 and the shortfall appears
paper/sec_intro.tex:131:        as recomputation, 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens; with a host tier it holds
paper/sec_intro.tex-132-        at 90.2--92.4\% over 1.25 to 1.73, while the \emph{device} misses more
paper/sec_intro.tex-133-        (\S\ref{sec:model}). Two configurations at the same running count and
paper/sec_intro.tex-134-        the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound, so it
paper/sec_intro.tex-135-        is not how full the pool is but what happens when it overflows; that
paper/sec_intro.tex:136:        pair differs in date and GPU as well, so a capacity control that
paper/sec_intro.tex:137:        varies the pool alone on one card is queued; \S\ref{sec:results}
paper/sec_intro.tex-138-        reports it when it lands. The per-step elasticity follows the same line:
paper/sec_intro.tex-139-        it passes one above $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$
paper/sec_intro.tex-140-        device-only and stays below one across the controllers' whole
paper/sec_intro.tex:141:        operating range with a host tier (\S\ref{sec:criterion}). Two
paper/sec_intro.tex-142-        measurements that do not share a code path---client-side step timing
paper/sec_intro.tex-143-        and engine-side token accounting---give the same answer. This is the
paper/sec_intro.tex-144-        finding we would carry to another system first, and it is what the
paper/sec_intro.tex-145-        title's question turns on.
paper/sec_intro.tex-146-  \item We test four routes by which an adaptive admission controller was
paper/sec_intro.tex-147-        expected to beat a tuned static cap on this workload, and we are
paper/sec_intro.tex-148-        precise about what each one rests on. Two are falsified by measurement
paper/sec_intro.tex-149-        against criteria we fixed before the runs---changing the accounting
paper/sec_intro.tex-150-        base (\S\ref{sec:results}) and changing the growth reserve. One is a
paper/sec_intro.tex-151-        knob rather than a hypothesis, and we report it only because it slides
--
paper/sec_intro.tex-153-        changing the execution granularity from session count to
paper/sec_intro.tex-154-        running-request count, is falsified by measurement \emph{and} by an
paper/sec_intro.tex-155-        argument about why the permit mechanism cannot express the control it
paper/sec_intro.tex-156-        would need. We do not call the last two of these falsifications of a
paper/sec_intro.tex-157-        stated hypothesis, because they were not.
paper/sec_intro.tex-158-  \item We give a two-part decomposition of the wall clock (\S\ref{sec:model})
paper/sec_intro.tex-159-        that locates the cancellation: admission policy moves the
paper/sec_intro.tex-160-        running-request count, and the per-step cost moves with it. On the
paper/sec_intro.tex-161-        device-only configurations we can account for that rise token for
paper/sec_intro.tex-162-        token---evicted prefixes are recomputed almost one for one. On
paper/sec_intro.tex:163:        host-tier configurations recomputation is flat and the account has to
paper/sec_intro.tex-164-        come from elsewhere; we report the part of that residual we were able
paper/sec_intro.tex-165-        to attribute, and the part we were not (\S\ref{sec:model},
paper/sec_intro.tex-166-        \S\ref{sec:repro}).
paper/sec_intro.tex-167-  \item We reduce the question to one measurable number, the elasticity of
paper/sec_intro.tex-168-        per-step cost with respect to the running count (\S\ref{sec:criterion}),
paper/sec_intro.tex-169-        and measure it at $0.38$ on the model-consistent step cost and $0.61$
paper/sec_intro.tex-170-        on the per-request one, on the configurations whose mechanism we can
paper/sec_intro.tex-171-        account for token for token. We offer it as a diagnostic rather than a
paper/sec_intro.tex-172-        predictor: it says where on the curve a policy operates and how much
paper/sec_intro.tex-173-        the remaining headroom is worth, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit
paper/sec_intro.tex-174-        about the sense in which it is circular and the sense in which it is
paper/sec_intro.tex-175-        not.
paper/sec_intro.tex-176-  \item We characterize when adaptivity \emph{could} have paid, and show that
paper/sec_intro.tex-177-        our workload is not that case: the same throttle configuration that is
paper/sec_intro.tex-178-        worth $+51\%$ at 25 sessions costs $-42\%$ at 200 (\S\ref{sec:load}).
paper/sec_intro.tex-179-        Put differently, no single scalar cap is optimal across both, which is
paper/sec_intro.tex:180:        the regime adaptive control exists for. On the low-load tier, where
paper/sec_intro.tex-181-        that argument bites hardest, the controller is \emph{not} worse---%
paper/sec_intro.tex-182-        same-generation measurements put it on the front, $+0.9$\,pp above the
paper/sec_intro.tex:183:        line and inside the noise. On the high-load tier it is dominated. That
paper/sec_intro.tex-184-        asymmetry, not a blanket failure, is what this paper measures.
paper/sec_intro.tex-185-  \item We make the measurements reproducible: the front and each controller's
paper/sec_intro.tex-186-        signed distance are produced by one script from the raw per-step logs,
paper/sec_intro.tex-187-        the tables and the sub-thousand-step runs by two others, all named in
paper/sec_intro.tex-188-        \S\ref{sec:repro}. We report the six claims we had to retract along the
paper/sec_intro.tex-189-        way, with the checks that caught them.
paper/sec_intro.tex-190-\end{itemize}
paper/sec_intro.tex-191-
paper/sec_intro.tex-192-\subsection{What we do not claim}
paper/sec_intro.tex-193-
paper/sec_intro.tex-194-We do not claim that admission control is useless in general. We claim it does
paper/sec_intro.tex:195:not pay \emph{here} and we show the frontier measurement that decides that; the
paper/sec_intro.tex-196-diagnostic of \S\ref{sec:criterion} is a cheaper check an engineer can run
paper/sec_intro.tex-197-before committing to a sweep, not a verdict on its own, and it can be taken on another
paper/sec_intro.tex-198-workload before committing to a sweep. We also do not claim our hardware and model are
paper/sec_intro.tex-199-representative; our results are from one 24\,GB accelerator and one 7B model,
paper/sec_intro.tex-200-and extending them is future work.
--

paper/sec_results.tex-183-
paper/sec_results.tex-184-Before the routes, one measurement that reframes all of them. Our controller
paper/sec_results.tex-185-keeps every session's permit for its lifetime, so its pool occupancy is a
paper/sec_results.tex-186-policy output, not a constraint. It runs at a median of 49.2\% of the pool on
paper/sec_results.tex:187:the base configuration, and the median number of sessions queueing for
paper/sec_results.tex-188-admission is 66. The configuration that finishes fastest---the repeat of that same static cap
paper/sec_results.tex-189-of four, at 209.1 minutes---runs at 71.9\%, and its first run at 70.2\%. Occupancy
paper/sec_results.tex-190-and running requests track each other reliably across the arms we have (the two
paper/sec_results.tex-191-columns in \texttt{occupancy.csv} rank the twenty-three arms we have almost
paper/sec_results.tex-192-identically, Spearman $\rho=0.95$), but
paper/sec_results.tex-193-running count does \emph{not} order wall clock: \texttt{horizon}=2 runs 1.25
paper/sec_results.tex-194-requests against the base arm's 1.14 (the all-samples mean, the column
paper/sec_results.tex-195-\texttt{occupancy.csv} carries; \S\ref{sec:model} uses the busy-conditional
paper/sec_results.tex-196-one) and finishes 15 minutes slower. That gap is
paper/sec_results.tex-197-what the next subsection is about.
--
paper/sec_results.tex-217-uses throughout. Two terms produce the gap: charging every
paper/sec_results.tex-218-live session its full context, when a parked session's KV is evictable and
paper/sec_results.tex-219-therefore absent from the engine's count, and the growth reserve, which alone
paper/sec_results.tex-220-is 26{,}762 tokens, 28\% of the budget.

paper/sec_results.tex-224-The first is that the controllers under-use the pool, and that this is a
paper/sec_results.tex-225-property of their estimates rather than of the hardware: the base
paper/sec_results.tex-226-configuration holds nearly half the pool idle (48.2\% of its budget, 50.8\% of\
paper/sec_results.tex:227:the pool) with 66 sessions queued, because
paper/sec_results.tex-228-it believes it has 7.6\% free when it has 48.2\%. Any account of why it is slow
paper/sec_results.tex-229-that does not mention this is incomplete.
paper/sec_results.tex-230-
paper/sec_results.tex-231-We then asked what actually stops it, by instrumenting the admission decision.

paper/sec_results.tex-235-session waiting and none admitted---the valve accounts for 6.8\%, the round gate
paper/sec_results.tex-236-for none, and a projection already above budget for 38.4\%.

paper/sec_results.tex-308-running requests reliably---\texttt{occupancy.csv}'s occupancy and
paper/sec_results.tex-309-running-count columns track
paper/sec_results.tex-310-each other across all twenty-three arms---but converting running requests into wall
paper/sec_results.tex-311-clock is not simply the per-step cost of \S\ref{sec:model}. Decomposing that
paper/sec_results.tex-312-19 minutes into its idle and busy components, 12.6 of them are idle time---%

paper/sec_results.tex-454-\subsection{Route 2: the growth reserve}
paper/sec_results.tex-455-
paper/sec_results.tex-456-Sessions grow by a median of 2.7K new tokens per round (\texttt{input\_len}
paper/sec_results.tex-457-over rounds $\ge 1$ in the trace), so the controller reserves
paper/sec_results.tex:458:capacity for the next \texttt{horizon} rounds. We swept the horizon over
paper/sec_results.tex-459-$\{0,1,2,3\}$ and the reserve is not a free parameter to be minimised:
paper/sec_results.tex-460-Fig.~\ref{fig:horizon} shows the response is non-monotone. Removing the
paper/sec_results.tex-461-reserve entirely costs 12.6\,pp of SLO, and a reserve of two rounds is
paper/sec_results.tex-462-15 minutes \emph{slower} than three. The optimum is at one round, and there it comes closest to the
paper/sec_results.tex-463-static front---0.93\,pp below it, inside the tie band---and that single point is

paper/sec_results.tex-507-We report two things honestly about this comparison. First, this is our
paper/sec_results.tex-508-reimplementation of a published control law, not the authors' system, and a
paper/sec_results.tex-509-reimplementation of any controller can underperform its paper. We built it to
paper/sec_results.tex-510-the published law, gave it the same permit interface and the same 2\,s cycle
paper/sec_results.tex-511-as \sys{}, and did not tune it beyond the two documented settings; but the
paper/sec_results.tex-512-caveat stands. Second, both controllers stall. At its paper parameters this one pinned its
paper/sec_results.tex-513-window at one session, admitted nothing, and left the engine idle for over an
paper/sec_results.tex-514-hour. Our own controller does the same thing more briefly: in 4 of the 11
paper/sec_results.tex-515-\sys{} runs a scan finds windows in which the engine was idle, 85 or more
--
paper/sec_results.tex-517-permit at all. Three of those runs have a single such window each---170.5, 173.2

paper/sec_results.tex-519-every throttle has nine of them, together 12\% of its wall clock (\texttt{stall.csv} states
paper/sec_results.tex-520-the criterion; an earlier draft quoted 150--175\,s and 0.9\% for all four,
paper/sec_results.tex-521-which no version of the criterion reproduces). The remaining runs, including
paper/sec_results.tex-522-the best one, show no such window. Nothing is logged
paper/sec_results.tex-523-as an error and the run recovers on its own.

paper/sec_results.tex-525-We traced one of those windows to a
paper/sec_results.tex-526-code path that explains it: single-flight admission considered only the head of
paper/sec_results.tex:527:the FIFO queue, and when the head was a session too large to fit the remaining
paper/sec_results.tex:528:budget it was never admitted, never left the queue, and blocked every smaller
paper/sec_results.tex-529-session behind it. That is the code every run in this paper executed. The fix
paper/sec_results.tex:530:in our repository---scan the queue, still admit at most one per cycle---is
paper/sec_results.tex-531-\emph{not} in the artifact that produced these results, so the numbers here
paper/sec_results.tex-532-predate it and we do not claim them for it. We are also explicit that this is
paper/sec_results.tex-533-\emph{a} path, not a proven cause:
paper/sec_results.tex-534-the audit is a log signature, and the same signature can be produced by other
paper/sec_results.tex-535-closed gates. What we can say is that the signature is present in a third of
paper/sec_results.tex-536-our \sys{} runs and absent from all of our static caps, which have no
paper/sec_results.tex-537-admission decision to get stuck in.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-2-\label{sec:load}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-3-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-4-A negative result about adaptivity invites the obvious objection: adaptivity
paper/sec_discussion.tex-5-pays when conditions \emph{change}, and a single stationary trace is the one
paper/sec_discussion.tex-6-case where a static cap should be near-optimal. We agree. What we test here is
paper/sec_discussion.tex:7:the weaker thing---two separate constant-load tiers---and we also ran the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-8-stronger one, a trace whose load moves mid-run, whose baselines co-tenancy made
paper/sec_discussion.tex-9-unusable; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports both what this section leaves open and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-10-why that experiment has no result to give.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-11-
paper/sec_discussion.tex:12:We run the same controller and the same cap family on a 25-session tier. The
paper/sec_discussion.tex-13-throttle configuration is reversed: with all safety throttles disabled, the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:14:25-session tier runs \emph{51\% faster} (58.4 to 28.8 minutes) at a cost of
paper/sec_discussion.tex:15:only 2.4\,pp of SLO, while on the 200-session tier disabling the same
paper/sec_discussion.tex-16-throttles costs 42\% (291.3 to 415.0 minutes) and 43.7\,pp of SLO. One switch,
paper/sec_discussion.tex-17-two regimes, opposite signs, both around 40--50\%
paper/sec_discussion.tex:18:(Fig.~\ref{fig:loadtier}). No single scalar setting is optimal across both.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-19-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-20-We flag the generation gap rather than let the figure imply more than we ran.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-22-earlier harness revision, and we never ran the all-throttles-off arm on the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:23:25-session tier under the current one. So the reversal is a direction we
paper/sec_discussion.tex-24-measured across two harness generations, not a same-generation paired
paper/sec_discussion.tex-25-comparison, and a reviewer should read it that way. The same-generation
paper/sec_discussion.tex:26:25-session measurement we do have is in Table~\ref{tab:tier25}, and it does not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-27-contain that arm.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-28-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-29-This is the regime in which adaptive control should win, and we now have the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-30-same-generation measurement that earlier drafts of this paper were missing.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-58-The finding is a trade, not a defeat, and it is the opposite of what the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:59:200-session tier shows. Against the same-generation envelope---which runs from
paper/sec_discussion.tex-60-\texttt{cap4} at (23.6, 79.2) through \texttt{cap3} at (24.2, 91.2) to
paper/sec_discussion.tex-61-\texttt{cap2} at (32.4, 92.8)---\sys{} sits $+0.9$\,pp above the line. A
paper/sec_discussion.tex-62-static cap of three is still 12\% faster in wall clock, but it gives up
paper/sec_discussion.tex:63:1.6\,pp of SLO to do it, so neither dominates the other. On this tier the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:64:controller is a different point on the front rather than a dominated one,
paper/sec_discussion.tex:65:which is exactly what the 200-session tier denies it.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-66-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-67-We are careful about the size, and about the yardstick. $+0.9$\,pp is
paper/sec_discussion.tex:68:measured against a straight-line interpolation between static points, not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-69-against a switching policy anyone ran, so the defensible statement is that
paper/sec_discussion.tex:70:\sys{} is not dominated by any static point we measured on this tier---not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-71-that it beats what a static mix could achieve. On 125 requests one request is
paper/sec_discussion.tex:72:worth 0.8\,pp, so the margin is about one request. The tier's
paper/sec_discussion.tex-73-segment-to-segment SD (5.1 to 21.4\,pp) is not the right yardstick: segment
paper/sec_discussion.tex-74-volatility is not run-to-run uncertainty. The right one is the replicate spread
paper/sec_discussion.tex-75-we have---which, across the five configurations we repeated, is 0.5, 0.8, 1.0,
paper/sec_discussion.tex-76-3.9 and 5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). Against the two tightest of those $+0.9$\,pp is about a replicate
paper/sec_discussion.tex-77-wide; against the three widest---which belong to the configurations that decide
paper/sec_discussion.tex-78-our headline elsewhere---it is inside one. We quote the tightest pair
paper/sec_discussion.tex-79-because it is the least favourable to us, not because it is the whole
paper/sec_discussion.tex-80-distribution. What we will defend is the weaker claim: on the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:81:low-load tier the controller is not worse, and the same switch that is worth
paper/sec_discussion.tex-82-$+51\%$ there costs $-42\%$ at high load. A controller that is competitive
paper/sec_discussion.tex:83:where load is light and dominated where load is heavy is the pattern this
paper/sec_discussion.tex-84-paper set out to explain, and it is the pattern we measured.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-85-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-86-The second finding is the more useful one. The signal an adaptive controller
paper/sec_discussion.tex-87-would need is available---the number of sessions waiting for admission is four
paper/sec_discussion.tex:88:times larger on the 200-session tier than on the same-generation 25-session arm
paper/sec_discussion.tex-89-(median 66 against 16, maximum 135 against 25; the earlier-harness 25-session
paper/sec_discussion.tex-90-arms of \S\ref{sec:load} read 9--11, which is where the 6--7$\times$ of an
paper/sec_discussion.tex-91-earlier draft came from, and we quote the same-generation pair)---but its
paper/sec_discussion.tex:92:\emph{interpretation} is not obvious. At the 25-session tier the controller
paper/sec_discussion.tex-93-holds sixteen sessions back while running one; that backlog is manufactured by
paper/sec_discussion.tex-94-the throttling itself rather than
paper/sec_discussion.tex-95-evidence that demand has slackened. Reading it correctly requires combining
paper/sec_discussion.tex-96-instantaneous backlog with progress through the run, which is a stateful
paper/sec_discussion.tex-97-computation a cap cannot perform. We regard this as the most promising
paper/sec_discussion.tex-98-direction we did not finish, and we are explicit that we did not finish it.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-112-\section{Related Work}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-113-\label{sec:related}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-114-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-115-\paragraph{Admission and scheduling for agentic serving.} Recent systems
paper/sec_discussion.tex-116-report large gains from treating an agent session, rather than a request, as
paper/sec_discussion.tex-117-the schedulable unit, and from preserving session state across tool calls:
paper/sec_discussion.tex-118-session-aware admission with CPU-restorable KV
paper/sec_discussion.tex-119-\cite{talaria,continuum}, program-level pause/resume with shortest-context-first
paper/sec_discussion.tex-120-restoration \cite{thunderagent}, and congestion-based control over an
paper/sec_discussion.tex-121-agent-level window \cite{concur}. A recurring measurement in this line is that
paper/sec_discussion.tex:122:recomputation, not admission, dominates: \cite{concur} attributes 49.1\% of
paper/sec_discussion.tex-123-end-to-end latency to recomputation, and \cite{mori} preserves state on the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-124-CPU at the cost of a reload. Our results are consistent with this literature
paper/sec_discussion.tex-125-rather than in tension with it, and \S\ref{sec:discussion} explains why: the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-126-mechanism these systems add is one our controller already has.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-127-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-128-\paragraph{Engine-side techniques.} A separate line improves the engine
paper/sec_discussion.tex-129-rather than the admission decision. Iteration-level batching and paged KV
paper/sec_discussion.tex-130-\cite{yu2022orca,kwon2023pagedattention} are the substrate every policy here runs
paper/sec_discussion.tex-131-on; chunked prefill with stall-free batching \cite{sarathi}, prefill/decode
paper/sec_discussion.tex-132-disaggregation \cite{distserve}, and cache-centric scheduling
paper/sec_discussion.tex-133-\cite{mooncake} are the refinements. Our \S\ref{sec:model} measures 2.6--8.2\,ms of each
paper/sec_discussion.tex-134-27--37\,ms step as work that is not decoding---prefill and non-decode
paper/sec_discussion.tex:135:overhead---which is exactly what this line attacks. We expect these techniques to move the frontier rather than to decide who
paper/sec_discussion.tex-136-sits above it, but we did not run the interaction and so state it as an
paper/sec_discussion.tex-137-expectation.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-138-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-139-\paragraph{State retention and preemption.} A third line keeps session state
paper/sec_discussion.tex-140-alive across the gaps we measure in \S\ref{sec:model}: keeping KV on cheaper
paper/sec_discussion.tex-141-storage across turns \cite{yu2025pensieve,gao2024cachedattention}, migrating it
paper/sec_discussion.tex-142-between instances \cite{sun2024llumnix}, and preempting at iteration granularity
paper/sec_discussion.tex-143-\cite{wu2026fastserve}. These systems exercise finer control than our 2\,s
paper/sec_discussion.tex-144-admission cycle, which is the boundary of our negative result: we falsify one
paper/sec_discussion.tex-145-control granularity, not the family. What they share with \sys{} is that the
--
paper/sec_discussion.tex-148-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-149-\paragraph{Application-level dependencies and replay.} Serving an LLM
paper/sec_discussion.tex-150-\emph{application} rather than a request means the scheduler sees dependencies
paper/sec_discussion.tex-151-between calls \cite{lin2024parrot}, and agentic traces have structure that
paper/sec_discussion.tex-152-request-level benchmarks do not capture \cite{wang2026xperf}. Our replay
paper/sec_discussion.tex-153-preserves that structure but fixes it in advance, which is exactly the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-154-limitation these works would press on.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-155-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-156-\paragraph{Prefix caching.} The prefix cache is often proposed as the signal
paper/sec_discussion.tex-157-an admission policy should use. What we measure is that the \emph{realized}
paper/sec_discussion.tex:158:reuse is a function of the admission policy and of the host tier, while the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-159-\emph{planned} reuse is a constant of the trace; an earlier draft of this
paper/sec_discussion.tex-160-paper conflated the two and got the direction wrong in both. The trace plans
paper/sec_discussion.tex-161-a round-1-and-later hit rate of 80.9\%. Without HiCache the realized
paper/sec_discussion.tex-162-round-1+ rate follows the plan only at the loosest cap and then collapses:
paper/sec_discussion.tex-163-80.8\%, 78.5\%, 70.6\%, 56.0\%, 32.8\% for caps of one through five. With
paper/sec_discussion.tex-164-HiCache it tracks the plan at every cap we ran: 80.7\%, 79.6\%, 78.7\% for
paper/sec_discussion.tex-165-caps of two, three and four. Our controller sits at 80.6\%.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-166-So the honest statement is not that admission does not move this quantity---%
paper/sec_discussion.tex:167:\texttt{cap4} alone moves it 25 points---but that the host tier is what keeps
paper/sec_discussion.tex-168-it from moving, which is the same mechanism \S\ref{sec:model} and
paper/sec_discussion.tex:169:\S\ref{sec:criterion} keep running into. A configuration without a host tier
paper/sec_discussion.tex:170:that admits four sessions does not have the cache that a host-tier one at the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-171-same concurrency does. All eight numbers in this paragraph are the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-172-\emph{observed} per-step hit rate (\texttt{server\_prefix\_hit\_rate} in the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-173-per-step logs), unweighted mean over rounds $\ge 1$; the planned rate is the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-174-trace constant 80.9\% and does not move with the cap. \S\ref{sec:model} uses
paper/sec_discussion.tex-175-a different convention---all steps, token-weighted, so that reuse and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-176-recomputation partition the prompt---under which the same collapse reads
paper/sec_discussion.tex-177-92.5\%$\to$36.4\% of the trace's bound for the device-only caps and
paper/sec_discussion.tex:178:92.4\%$\to$90.2\% for the static host-tier caps. We state both because the paper's earlier
paper/sec_discussion.tex-179-drafts mixed them. We note the boundary of this claim---we varied admission policy,
paper/sec_discussion.tex-180-not eviction policy, and SGLang exposes alternate eviction policies we did not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-181-test. A characterization of production KV-cache traffic at a large cloud
paper/sec_discussion.tex-182-provider finds that eviction-policy design is ``highly workload-dependent''
paper/sec_discussion.tex-183-and that reuse patterns differ sharply by request category
paper/sec_discussion.tex-184-\cite{wang2025kvcachewild}; that is reason to expect a single trace family to
paper/sec_discussion.tex-185-understate the space, though it is not a policy comparison and we do not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-186-present it as one.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-187-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-188-\section{Discussion}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-189-\label{sec:discussion}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-190-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-191-\paragraph{Why published gains do not reappear.} The systems cited above win
paper/sec_discussion.tex-192-by keeping session state alive across tool waits and by pausing at agent
paper/sec_discussion.tex-193-granularity rather than request granularity. \sys{} has both properties by
paper/sec_discussion.tex-194-construction: the permit file gives per-session pause and resume, and the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:195:controller never frees capacity by discarding a session---it admits fewer
paper/sec_discussion.tex-196-sessions instead. To that extent the mechanism these systems add is one our
paper/sec_discussion.tex-197-controller already has, which is one reason adding admission intelligence on
paper/sec_discussion.tex-198-top of it buys nothing here.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-199-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-200-We are careful not to overstate this, because the distinction is between
paper/sec_discussion.tex-201-\emph{choosing} to discard state and \emph{having} it evicted. Retention here
paper/sec_discussion.tex-202-is the engine's radix cache doing its ordinary job; it is not something the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-203-controller implements, and \S\ref{sec:model} measures that the cache evicts
paper/sec_discussion.tex-204-parked prefixes under pressure and that they are recomputed. What \sys{}
paper/sec_discussion.tex:205:avoids is the deliberate trade of state for capacity. A system that manages the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:206:host tier as an explicit policy---as \cite{mori} and \cite{yu2025pensieve}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-207-do---holds a lever we do not, and our negative result does not cover it.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-208-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-209-\paragraph{The threshold decides the verdict, and we report that.} Every SLO
paper/sec_discussion.tex-210-number in this paper counts a request as good if its time-to-first-token is
paper/sec_discussion.tex-211-under 10\,s. That constant sets the whole vertical axis, so we re-derived the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:212:frontier at 5\,s and 20\,s as well, from the same per-request logs. The
paper/sec_discussion.tex-213-ordering of the static caps does not change. What changes is where the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-214-controllers sit relative to them, and at the loose end one of them crosses:
paper/sec_discussion.tex-215-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-216-\begin{center}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-217-\small
paper/sec_discussion.tex-218-\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-219-\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
paper/sec_discussion.tex-220-  \toprule
paper/sec_discussion.tex-221-  SLO threshold & 5\,s & 10\,s & 20\,s \\
paper/sec_discussion.tex-222-  \midrule
--
paper/sec_discussion.tex-257-submitted yet, so the wait is invisible---and that wait is precisely the cost
paper/sec_discussion.tex-258-an admission controller imposes. We therefore recomputed the runs we have per-step
paper/sec_discussion.tex-259-logs for on a ready-to-first-token basis, where a round's clock starts when the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-260-session is actually able to run (its arrival for round~0, the previous round's
paper/sec_discussion.tex-261-completion plus its tool wait otherwise). That is nineteen of the twenty-five
paper/sec_discussion.tex-262-200-session runs in Table~\ref{tab:runs}; absent are the no-control baseline,
paper/sec_discussion.tex-263-the two HiCache anchor repeats and three controller repeats, and we do not
paper/sec_discussion.tex-264-extend the claim to them.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-265-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-266-The correction is large. Every configuration loses 2.9--19.3\,pp of SLO, and the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:267:loss is systematic: tight caps queue the hardest, so \texttt{cap1} falls from
paper/sec_discussion.tex-268-94.8\% to 76.2\% while \texttt{cap5} falls only from 27.1\% to 24.2\%. A
paper/sec_discussion.tex-269-session admitted last under \texttt{cap1} can wait hours for its turn; its

paper/sec_discussion.tex-312-\paragraph{Limitations.} Our results come from one 24\,GB accelerator and one
paper/sec_discussion.tex-313-7B model. The workload axis we did test once more (\S\ref{sec:xtrace}); the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-314-hardware axis we did not, so generality across devices is untested. Only five configurations
paper/sec_discussion.tex-315-have been repeated---four of them twice and the base configuration four
paper/sec_discussion.tex-316-times---so the replicate spread we report in \S\ref{sec:results} is estimated
paper/sec_discussion.tex-317-from one to three degrees of freedom per configuration; a margin as small as the one that decides our verdict would need several
paper/sec_discussion.tex-318-more repeats per configuration to resolve, and we did not run them. Two specific boundaries
paper/sec_discussion.tex-319-matter. First, the pool holds about four median sessions, so concurrency is
paper/sec_discussion.tex:320:capacity-bound by construction; on a device with a much larger pool the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-321-premise may not hold and the conclusion may invert---we regard reproducing on
paper/sec_discussion.tex-322-a larger device as the single most important next experiment. Second, the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-323-load is stationary, single-tenant and single-SLO. The load-dependence result
paper/sec_discussion.tex:324:of \S\ref{sec:load} is measured on two \emph{separate} constant-load tiers, so
paper/sec_discussion.tex-325-it says what to expect when conditions differ, not what happens while they
paper/sec_discussion.tex-326-change. We did run the experiment that would settle that---a trace whose load
paper/sec_discussion.tex:327:moves mid-run, with a static cap on each tier and a switcher that changes its
paper/sec_discussion.tex-328-cap at the trace's designed switch point---and we cannot report a result from
paper/sec_discussion.tex-329-it, in either direction, because its static baselines were measured under
paper/sec_discussion.tex-330-host co-tenancy: \texttt{var\_cap3} spent 158 of its 266 minutes at four or
paper/sec_discussion.tex-331-more concurrent engine instances, and \texttt{var\_cap2} is flagged as a
paper/sec_discussion.tex-332-watch (\S\ref{sec:repro}). A first look at those runs has the switcher
paper/sec_discussion.tex-333-finishing 15 minutes ahead of the best static cap at an SLO inside the tie
paper/sec_discussion.tex-334-band, which would be the first evidence in this paper that adaptivity pays;
paper/sec_discussion.tex-335-we do not report it as a result, because the baseline it beats is precisely
paper/sec_discussion.tex-336-the one the audit flags, and a 60\%-contaminated baseline is inflated in
paper/sec_discussion.tex-337-exactly the direction that manufactures that finding. Clean re-runs of that
paper/sec_discussion.tex-338-family are in flight, and the honest position until they land is that the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-339-variable-load case is measured and unusable, not unmeasured. Multi-tenant and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-340-heterogeneous-SLO settings are where we would look next.

paper/sec_model.tex-97-\paragraph{Method 1 (derived).} $\tau_1 = W_{\text{busy}} \cdot \overline{R} /
paper/sec_model.tex-98-G$. Two choices in that expression are easy to get wrong and both matter. The
paper/sec_model.tex-99-time term must be the \emph{busy} component, not the wall clock---using the
paper/sec_model.tex-100-wall clock charges idle time to the steps. And $\overline{R}$ must be the mean
paper/sec_model.tex-101-running count \emph{during busy time}, not the mean over the whole run: the
paper/sec_model.tex-102-whole-run mean includes the idle samples, where the count is zero, so it equals
paper/sec_model.tex-103-$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \times$ (busy fraction). Using it makes $\tau_1$
paper/sec_model.tex-104-too low by exactly the busy fraction---so the estimate is short by the
paper/sec_model.tex:105:\emph{idle} fraction, 36.3\% for \texttt{cap1}, whose busy share is 63.7\%. We report both columns in the data, and use the
paper/sec_model.tex-106-busy-conditional mean throughout; the whole-run mean is what an earlier
paper/sec_model.tex-107-revision of this paper used, and it made the two methods agree far better than
paper/sec_model.tex-108-they do.
paper/sec_model.tex-109-
paper/sec_model.tex-110-\paragraph{Method 2 (independent).} For each step record we compute the
paper/sec_model.tex-111-per-request decode interval,
paper/sec_model.tex-112-$(\text{total\_duration} - \text{first\_token}) / (\text{output\_len} - 1)$,

paper/sec_model.tex-162-
paper/sec_model.tex-163-Equation~\ref{eq:busy} would be uninteresting if $\tau$ were a constant. It is
paper/sec_model.tex-164-not. Across the thirteen runs we fitted---the five device-only caps, the three
paper/sec_model.tex-165-host-tier caps that span the same range, and the five controller arms we report
paper/sec_model.tex:166:individually---$\tau_1$ ranges from 27.0 to 36.7\,ms and $\tau_2$
paper/sec_model.tex-167-from 20.6 to 33.1\,ms, and both rise with the running count: fitting
paper/sec_model.tex-168-Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} to the five device-only configurations gives
paper/sec_model.tex:169:$\varepsilon = 0.38$ for $\tau_1$ and $0.61$ for $\tau_2$
paper/sec_model.tex-170-($R^2 = 0.89$ for the latter, Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
paper/sec_model.tex-171-
paper/sec_model.tex-172-The relation is a trend, not a law. Two host-tier configurations with similar
paper/sec_model.tex:173:running counts behave differently: \texttt{cap4\_hc} runs 1.73 requests at
paper/sec_model.tex-174-29.8\,ms while \texttt{F:hz0} runs 1.66 at 35.7\,ms. Running count sets a
paper/sec_model.tex-175-floor and a trend, not a value---and on the host-tier family, as we show below,
paper/sec_model.tex-176-it does not set the value at all.
paper/sec_model.tex-177-
paper/sec_model.tex-178-This is the crux of the paper, and we state it at the strength the arithmetic
paper/sec_model.tex:179:supports. Hold $\tau_1$ at its value for \texttt{cap1}, 28.8\,ms, and busy
paper/sec_model.tex-180-time falls as $1/\overline{R}$: raising the running count from 1.00 to 1.84
paper/sec_model.tex-181-should have cut busy time by a factor of 1.84, from 340 minutes to 184. The
paper/sec_model.tex-182-measured curve flattens and lands at 234. The rise in $\tau$ takes back 50 of
paper/sec_model.tex-183-the 156 minutes that the extra concurrency would otherwise have bought---\emph{a
paper/sec_model.tex-184-third} of the gain, not all of it and, as an earlier revision of this paper
paper/sec_model.tex-185-claimed, not more than half. The other two thirds are real: raising
paper/sec_model.tex-186-concurrency does make the engine faster. What it does not do is make it faster
paper/sec_model.tex-187-in proportion, and the shortfall compounds with every controller that tries to
paper/sec_model.tex-188-exploit the difference.
paper/sec_model.tex-189-
paper/sec_model.tex-190-\paragraph{What the engine is doing when it is not decoding.} SGLang exposes
paper/sec_model.tex-191-two counters that answer this directly: tokens evicted from the radix cache,
paper/sec_model.tex-192-and prompt tokens that had to be prefilled rather than reused. Across the five
paper/sec_model.tex-193-static caps \emph{without} HiCache the two track each other almost exactly.
paper/sec_model.tex:194:\texttt{cap1} evicts 9.2\,M tokens and prefills 8.6\,M uncached;
paper/sec_model.tex-195-\texttt{cap5} evicts 21.3\,M and prefills 20.7\,M; the gap is 0.6--0.7\,M on
paper/sec_model.tex-196-every one of the five. An evicted prefix is recomputed, close to one token for
paper/sec_model.tex-197-one, and that recomputation is chunked into decode steps. Those same five runs
paper/sec_model.tex-198-give recomputation rising with $\tau$ across four of the five points: 9.2\,M
paper/sec_model.tex-199-uncached tokens at 27.4\,ms, 11.5 at 29.7, 15.4 at 32.4, 20.7 at 36.7. The
paper/sec_model.tex:200:exception is the first point, \texttt{cap1}, which recomputes least (8.6\,M)
paper/sec_model.tex-201-and still costs 28.8\,ms---more than \texttt{cap2} does for more recomputation.
paper/sec_model.tex-202-We report the ordering we have rather than rounding it to a monotone one: it is
paper/sec_model.tex-203-a mechanism consistent with the $\tau$ trend on four of the five device-only
paper/sec_model.tex-204-configurations, and one point it does not order. It is five points on five
paper/sec_model.tex-205-different policies, not an
paper/sec_model.tex-206-intervention: we did not hold concurrency fixed and vary eviction, so we call
paper/sec_model.tex-207-it consistent rather than demonstrated.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-352-guarded against a shrunken KV pool.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-353-
paper/sec_discussion.tex-354-\emph{Co-tenancy on the measurement host.} Our runs share a host with other
paper/sec_discussion.tex-355-tenants and with each other, and late in the campaign we found that this
paper/sec_discussion.tex-356-matters more than the pool guard catches. An audit
paper/sec_discussion.tex-357-(\texttt{gen\_concurrency.py}, data in \texttt{paper/data/concurrency.csv})
paper/sec_discussion.tex-358-reconstructs, for every run, how many other runs were alive for how long. Runs
paper/sec_discussion.tex-359-that overlapped three or more others for at least an hour are flagged;
paper/sec_discussion.tex-360-fourteen of ours are, two of which are failed runs we do not report. The flag is a risk marker, not a measurement of damage,
paper/sec_discussion.tex-361-and we treat it that way: five of the twelve (\texttt{F:t85}, \texttt{F:t90},
paper/sec_discussion.tex:362:\texttt{var\_foyer}, \texttt{cap1} and the published-parameter AIMD row) show
paper/sec_discussion.tex-363-no aggregate inflation at all, because their overlap was short relative to the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-364-run or did not touch their decode interval. Where the damage is legible it is large---the
paper/sec_discussion.tex:365:per-request decode interval $\tau_2$, measured client-side and never touching
paper/sec_discussion.tex-366-the sampler, sits at 30.9--33.3\,ms for the three affected arms still carrying
paper/sec_discussion.tex-367-the flag in
paper/sec_discussion.tex-368-Table~\ref{tab:runs} against 23.7--25.0\,ms across the seven clean runs of the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-369-same controller family---a 24 to 41\% inflation. A fourth affected arm has
paper/sec_discussion.tex-370-since been re-measured alone and came in at
paper/sec_discussion.tex-371-24.8\,ms, inside the clean range, which is the correction in action. Two
paper/sec_discussion.tex-372-controls pin the cause to co-tenancy rather than to the configuration, and both
paper/sec_discussion.tex-373-are within-run---the same run, the same policy, the same trace, with the only
paper/sec_discussion.tex:374:change being who else was on the machine. One run (\texttt{cap1}) reads a
paper/sec_discussion.tex:375:per-half-hour median $\tau_2$ of 20.3--20.9\,ms for its first five and a half
paper/sec_discussion.tex-376-hours, and 21.3, 27.1 and 21.1 in the three half-hours after two others start.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-377-Two runs move the other way as neighbours leave: \texttt{F:t85} from 29.8\,ms
paper/sec_discussion.tex-378-in the half-hour before to 22.3--22.6 in the four after, \texttt{F:rl99} from
paper/sec_discussion.tex-379-32.4 to 25.2 and 25.1. We chose the half-hour window because it is the widest
paper/sec_discussion.tex-380-one that resolves the transition and still puts at least twenty steps in every
paper/sec_discussion.tex-381-bucket quoted; the finer series is noisier and does not support a
paper/sec_discussion.tex-382-minute-resolution claim, so we do not make one.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-383-(\texttt{tau2\_windows.csv} carries the series.) Fourteen of our runs fall in the flagged class; five of them are rows of
paper/sec_discussion.tex:384:Table~\ref{tab:runs} (\texttt{cap1}, \texttt{F:hz0},
paper/sec_discussion.tex-385-\texttt{F:hz2}, \texttt{F:breshz1}, and the published-parameter AIMD row) and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-386-nine are arms we do not report here. The flag is a window, and only three of
paper/sec_discussion.tex:387:those five are actually damaged---the controller variants whose $\tau_2$
paper/sec_discussion.tex:388:is 30.9--33.3\,ms. \texttt{cap1} and the AIMD row show no aggregate
paper/sec_discussion.tex-389-inflation (20.6 and 24.4\,ms), so we keep their numbers and flag the window.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-390-A sixth row carried the flag and the damage, and has been re-measured alone
paper/sec_discussion.tex-391-under the same one-run-per-GPU discipline: \texttt{bres}, whose clean
paper/sec_discussion.tex:392:$\tau_2$ is 24.8\,ms. Its row in Table~\ref{tab:runs} now carries the clean
paper/sec_discussion.tex-393-numbers, and the two conclusions that rested on it---its step-cost

paper/sec_discussion.tex-398-columns as unusable: the signed distances in Table~\ref{tab:runs} for those

paper/sec_discussion.tex-453-\emph{The denominator of the step cost.} Our first \S\ref{sec:model} divided by

--
paper/sec_discussion.tex-476-front. We expected the reason to be a cancellation---admission policy controls
paper/sec_discussion.tex-477-how many requests are in flight, the per-step cost rises with that number, and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-478-the product is what the wall clock sees---and we built a two-part decomposition
paper/sec_discussion.tex-479-of the wall clock that makes the cancellation measurable. That decomposition
paper/sec_discussion.tex-480-holds, and the cost it measures is real, but it is not the whole reason and on
paper/sec_discussion.tex-481-the configuration our controllers run it is not the binding one: there the
paper/sec_discussion.tex-482-elasticity stays below one across the operating range, so added concurrency was
paper/sec_discussion.tex-483-still cheap and the engine had room. Our controllers did not take it. What we
paper/sec_discussion.tex-484-can say at the end is narrower than what we set out to say: on this hardware and
paper/sec_discussion.tex-485-this workload, the admission rules we could build and tune did not beat a static
paper/sec_discussion.tex:486:cap, the capacity estimate they act on is 1.79$\times$ the truth, and a
paper/sec_discussion.tex-487-structural idle component that no policy we built recovers at a profit remains.
paper/sec_discussion.tex-488-The regime in which adaptivity should have paid---where the same switch is worth
paper/sec_discussion.tex-489-$+51\%$ and $-42\%$---is one where the controller is not dominated by any static
paper/sec_discussion.tex-490-point we measured, and does not dominate one either. The measurement we offer is
paper/sec_discussion.tex-491-a diagnostic an engineer can take before committing to a sweep; whether it
paper/sec_discussion.tex-492-predicts the outcome on another workload is a question we tested once and could
paper/sec_discussion.tex-493-not confirm.

paper/sec_discussion.tex-495-The part we would carry elsewhere is not the negative result but the

--
paper/sec_criterion.tex-15-the two cancel exactly and it has none. Everything in between is a

paper/sec_criterion.tex-17-
paper/sec_criterion.tex-18-On the five device-only configurations, where \S\ref{sec:model} establishes
paper/sec_criterion.tex-19-that the step cost is driven by recomputation, $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$
paper/sec_criterion.tex-20-runs from 1.00 to 1.84. A least-squares fit of Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} over
paper/sec_criterion.tex-21-those five points gives $\epsilon = 0.61$ on the per-request measurement
paper/sec_criterion.tex-22-($R^2 = 0.89$), or $0.38$ on the model-consistent one (Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
paper/sec_criterion.tex-23-
paper/sec_criterion.tex-24-That fit is a straight line through a curve, and the curve is what matters. The
paper/sec_criterion.tex:25:segment elasticities are $\epsilon = -0.21$ from \texttt{cap1} to
paper/sec_criterion.tex-26-\texttt{cap2}, $+0.43$ from \texttt{cap2} to \texttt{cap3}, $+1.15$ from
paper/sec_criterion.tex-27-\texttt{cap3} to \texttt{cap4}, and $+1.14$ from \texttt{cap4} to
paper/sec_criterion.tex-28-\texttt{cap5}. Below $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$ added
paper/sec_criterion.tex-29-concurrency still buys something; above it, $\epsilon$ passes one, which means
paper/sec_criterion.tex-30-the engine gets \emph{slower} in wall clock as more requests run. The single
paper/sec_criterion.tex-31-number $0.38$ averages those regimes and describes none of them.
paper/sec_criterion.tex-32-
paper/sec_criterion.tex-33-The operating region is the one that matters, and it is not the region the
paper/sec_criterion.tex-34-controllers run in. All nine controller arms run with HiCache enabled, which
paper/sec_criterion.tex-35-the five device-only caps above do not. Measuring the same elasticity on the
paper/sec_criterion.tex-36-three HiCache configurations we have gives $\epsilon = +0.19$ from
paper/sec_criterion.tex-37-\texttt{cap2}+HC to \texttt{cap3}+HC and $+0.46$ from \texttt{cap3}+HC to
paper/sec_criterion.tex:38:\texttt{cap4}+HC, over $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.25 to 1.73. That range
paper/sec_criterion.tex-39-covers the whole controller operating region (1.35 to 1.69), and over it

paper/sec_model.tex-228-\paragraph{Where the mechanism stops.} With HiCache enabled the
paper/sec_model.tex-229-correspondence breaks---and it breaks in a way that confirms the mechanism

paper/sec_model.tex:233:other 13.3\,M is served from the host tier. Across the nineteen host-tier
